- What to do if your inner voice is cruel | Ethan Kross for Big Think+: Kind: captions Language: en - I think chatter is one of the big problems we face as a species. We spend between one-third and one-half of our waking hours not living in the present. And what do we do during that time? We're talking to ourselves. Your inner voice is your ability to silently use language to reflect on your life. Chatter refers to the dark side of the inner voice. When we turn our attention inward to make sense of our problems, we don't end up finding solutions. We end up ruminating, worrying, catastrophizing. We get stuck in a negative cycle that takes this remarkable tool that we possess, this inner voice, and it turns it into a curse rather than a blessing. My name's Ethan Kross, I'm a professor of psychology and management, and I'm the author of the book, "Chatter: The Voice in Our Head, Why It Matters, and How to Harness It." In terms of whether there's an evolutionary purpose to the inner voice, many scientists believe that language is a tool that helps us navigate the world, and our ability to use language not only to communicate with others, but to communicate with ourselves, provides us with a survival advantage. It's an incredible problem-solving device. At the most basic end of the spectrum, our inner voice is part of what we call our 'Verbal Working Memory System.' It's a basic feature of the human mind that helps us keep verbal information active in our heads. Our inner voice also lets us simulate and plan. So before a big presentation, I'll go over in my head what I'm gonna say, what are the different talking points that I'm gonna run through- I'll hear what questions the audience is gonna ask me, and then I'll respond in turn. I'm simulating that exchange, and I'm using my inner voice to help me do that. Our inner voice helps us control ourselves. Think about the last time you may have wanted to reach for a treat late at night, but then you think to yourself, "Don't do it, you'll regret it in the morning." That's your inner voice. And then finally, the inner voice helps us 'storify' our lives. Many of us turn our attention inward to come up with some narrative that explains our experiences in ways that give shape to our understanding of who we are, our identity. So sometimes this inner voice can be an incredible source of help, but at other times it can really sink us. First, it makes it incredibly hard for us to focus. Chatter consumes our attention. Chatter can also create friction in your relationships because you're talking about your problems over and over again, and not being a great listener to others. It can also make us more irritable, and lead to something called 'displaced aggression.' Finally, we know that chatter can have severe, negative, physical health effects. You've probably heard that stress kills- that's not exactly true. A stress response is a really adaptive response. What makes stress toxic is when it remains chronically-elevated over time. This is precisely what chatter does. We experience a stressor in our life, it then ends, but in our minds our chatter perpetuates it. We keep thinking about that event over and over and over again. And that keeps that stress response active in ways that can predict things like cardiovascular disease, chronic inflammation, and even cancer. It's hard to overstate how negative the implications of chatter can be. The good news is there is a science-based toolkit that you can use to regain control of your inner voice. There are things we can do on our own, ways of harnessing our relationships with other people, and even ways of interacting with physical environments. One really great example of these tools are rituals. We love having control. When you experience chatter, you often feel like your thoughts are in control of you. One of the things that we've learned through science is that we can compensate for this feeling out of control by creating order around us- rituals are one way to do that. A ritual is an ordered sequence of behaviors that you rigidly perform the same way each time by engaging the same sequence of behaviors every time the same way. That's giving you a sense of order and control. That can feel really good when you're mired in chatter. The tennis great, Rafael Nadal, he said the hardest thing that he struggles to do on the tennis court is manage the voices inside his head. He engages in rituals. During breaks between play he goes over to his bench. First, he takes a sip out of one water bottle. Then he takes a sip out of another. Then he puts each water bottle back exactly where he picked them up from on a diagonal to the court. It's a ritual he does to manage his chatter. There are no individual tools that work for all people in all situations to help manage your chatter. Instead, the real challenge is to figure out what are the unique combinations of tools that work best for you? Are you weaker for experiencing chatter? Absolutely not. You are human for experiencing chatter, so welcome to the human condition.

- How To Eliminate Microphone Feedback | 5 Must-Know Tips: Kind: captions Language: en Few things are capable of ruining a&nbsp; live performance like the screeching&nbsp;&nbsp; sound of microphone feedback.&nbsp; In this video, I'm teaching&nbsp;&nbsp; you a five-step process to eliminate&nbsp; microphone feedback before it happens,&nbsp;&nbsp; but if this is our first time meeting my&nbsp; name is Kyle. Welcome to Audio University. Anytime there's a microphone sending signal to a&nbsp; speaker in the same room, there's a chance that&nbsp;&nbsp; a feedback loop will be created. The signal can&nbsp; only be amplified up to a certain point before&nbsp;&nbsp; feedback will occur. The microphone signal is&nbsp; amplified through the speaker then the sound from&nbsp;&nbsp; the speaker is picked up by the microphone&nbsp; and amplified through the speaker again.&nbsp;&nbsp; Imagine a vocalist singing into a microphone&nbsp; on stage. The point at which the sound from&nbsp;&nbsp; the speaker becomes louder at the microphone than&nbsp; the sound from the vocalist, feedback will occur.&nbsp;&nbsp; The amount of amplification that's allowed&nbsp; before this point is called the maximum&nbsp;&nbsp; gain before feedback and by&nbsp; following these five steps,&nbsp;&nbsp; you'll drastically increase the maximum&nbsp; gain before feedback of your system. The most effective way to increase your system's&nbsp; maximum gain before feedback is to provide&nbsp;&nbsp; more space between the microphone and the&nbsp; speaker. The main speakers are the speakers&nbsp;&nbsp; that point toward the audience. It's best to&nbsp; get as much distance as possible between the&nbsp;&nbsp; microphones on stage and the main speakers. This&nbsp; will allow the sound to attenuate so much that&nbsp;&nbsp; it won't cause a feedback loop by&nbsp; the time it reaches the microphone.&nbsp;&nbsp; Stage monitors are the speakers on stage pointing&nbsp; toward the musicians. The signal from a microphone&nbsp;&nbsp; will be sent to probably a monitor wedge that will&nbsp; allow the vocalist to hear his or herself sing.&nbsp;&nbsp; It would be counterproductive to put the stage&nbsp; monitors as far away from the microphone as&nbsp;&nbsp; possible because the vocalist is right next to&nbsp; the microphone and that would just mean that&nbsp;&nbsp; the speaker has to be turned up more. Instead&nbsp; try to get the speaker as close to the vocalist&nbsp;&nbsp; and as on axis with the vocalist as possible so&nbsp; that less amplification is required. Remember that&nbsp;&nbsp; the closer a speaker is to the listener, the&nbsp; quieter it can be. That's why IEMs (or in-ear&nbsp;&nbsp; monitors) are so useful for live sound production&nbsp; because they can be inside the musician's ears&nbsp;&nbsp; and, therefore, can be extremely quiet while still&nbsp; providing adequate level to the person listening.&nbsp;&nbsp; Not only that, but if you can convince musicians&nbsp; to use in-ear monitors it will quiet down the&nbsp;&nbsp; level on stage which will probably lead to a&nbsp; cleaner, easier-to-mix front of house experience. Using directional microphones and speakers&nbsp; is another way to increase your system's&nbsp;&nbsp; maximum gain before feedback. This will&nbsp; allow the speakers and microphones to be&nbsp;&nbsp; in closer proximity to one another&nbsp; without causing a feedback loop.&nbsp;&nbsp; The most common polar pattern for microphones in&nbsp; live sound reinforcement is cardioid. One reason&nbsp;&nbsp; cardioid mics are so popular in live sound&nbsp; is that they reject the sound coming from the&nbsp;&nbsp; speakers and therefore increase the maximum gain&nbsp; before feedback of the system. When placing the&nbsp;&nbsp; microphones on stage, consider the microphone's&nbsp; polar pattern. This graph shows that cardioid mics&nbsp;&nbsp; reject sound best from the rear. If a microphone&nbsp; has a cardioid pattern such as the Shure SM58,&nbsp;&nbsp; in theory you should place it so that the&nbsp; rear rejection point faces toward the speaker. Depending on the size of the stage, the&nbsp; spacing between the microphones and speakers,&nbsp;&nbsp; and the types of microphones&nbsp; and speakers you're using,&nbsp;&nbsp; certain frequencies will tend to feedback&nbsp; sooner than others. For each instrument,&nbsp;&nbsp; set a high pass filter as high as you can&nbsp; without affecting the instrument's sound.&nbsp;&nbsp; To learn more about high pass filters, how to use&nbsp; them, and how they work you can watch this video.&nbsp;&nbsp; Ringing out speakers is the process of finding and&nbsp; eliminating feedback with EQ. Using an equalizer,&nbsp;&nbsp; you can eliminate the first frequencies that feed&nbsp; back without affecting the other frequencies.&nbsp;&nbsp; A graphic equalizer is a great tool for working&nbsp; quickly. It's important to understand that less&nbsp;&nbsp; is more during this process. If you follow these&nbsp; steps, you'll make sure that you're not doing more&nbsp;&nbsp; harm than good. First ensure that the vocal&nbsp; microphone preamp is set as low as possible.&nbsp;&nbsp; Next send the vocal microphone to&nbsp; the nearest monitor at unity gain.&nbsp;&nbsp; This means that the auxiliary send&nbsp; to the monitor is set to 0 dB.&nbsp;&nbsp; Start to bring up the microphone preamp slowly.&nbsp; When you hear a frequency starting to feedback,&nbsp;&nbsp; reduce that frequency by 6 dB on the graphic EQ.&nbsp; Repeat this process for the next four frequencies&nbsp;&nbsp; that feedback. Then do the same thing but only&nbsp; reduce the next few frequencies by 3 dB. After&nbsp;&nbsp; you've found about six frequencies, reduce the&nbsp; microphone preamp gain by 6 dB and speak into the&nbsp;&nbsp; microphone to determine if the microphone is loud&nbsp; enough without feeding back. It's really important&nbsp;&nbsp; that you don't overdo this. If you find yourself&nbsp; removing seven, eight, or more frequencies,&nbsp;&nbsp; you're effectively just turning everything down&nbsp; and degrading the frequency balance of the signal.&nbsp;&nbsp; Instead, reduce fewer frequencies with the EQ and&nbsp; just reduce the preamp gain you're applying to the&nbsp;&nbsp; signal. That will usually yield better results for&nbsp; both you mixing and for the musicians on stage. In a recording studio you sometimes set&nbsp; the preamp gain before anything else,&nbsp;&nbsp; but while mixing monitors I prefer to use as&nbsp; little preamp gain as possible. To accomplish&nbsp;&nbsp; this, I recommend setting all auxiliary master&nbsp; faders to unity or 0 dB. In general, each musician&nbsp;&nbsp; will want to hear their own instrument more than&nbsp; anyone else will. Start with the kick drum sent&nbsp;&nbsp; to the drummer at unity and set the preamp&nbsp; gain based on how much the drummer needs.&nbsp;&nbsp; Start with the guitar microphone sent to the&nbsp; guitarist at unity and set the preamp gain based&nbsp;&nbsp; on how much the guitarist needs, and so on. Once&nbsp; you've set the preamp gain for each instrument&nbsp;&nbsp; based on how much the musician playing that&nbsp; instrument needed in their monitor, go ahead and&nbsp;&nbsp; use the aux sends to the other monitors to give&nbsp; the other musicians what they need. The reason I&nbsp;&nbsp; make this recommendation is to avoid unnecessary&nbsp; amplification. If you add a ton of preamp&nbsp;&nbsp; gain only to send a tiny amount to the monitor&nbsp; wedges, then that preamp gain was unnecessary. How you place microphones on stage will&nbsp; play a large role in reducing feedback.&nbsp;&nbsp; Keeping the microphone in close proximity to&nbsp; the sound source will help reduce feedback. The&nbsp;&nbsp; further a vocalist is from their microphone,&nbsp; the more gain you'll need to use to get an&nbsp;&nbsp; adequate level. If the vocalist stays close to the&nbsp; microphone less gain will be required, reducing&nbsp;&nbsp; the chances of feedback. I already mentioned the&nbsp; microphone polar pattern earlier, but did you know&nbsp;&nbsp; that the way you hold the microphone can actually&nbsp; change the polar pattern of that microphone?&nbsp;&nbsp; A common mistake in microphone technique is to cup&nbsp; the microphone. This mistake can turn a cardioid&nbsp;&nbsp; microphone that picks up sound from the front into&nbsp; more of an omnidirectional microphone that picks&nbsp;&nbsp; up sound evenly from all directions. If you've&nbsp; got any experience working with musicians you&nbsp;&nbsp; know that you can only really control this up&nbsp; to a certain point. The musician's microphone&nbsp;&nbsp; technique will either make your job as an audio&nbsp; engineer much more difficult or much easier. And while you can't directly control the&nbsp; musicians, the equipment, or the environment,&nbsp;&nbsp; if you follow these steps you'll set yourself&nbsp; up for success. Here's another bonus tip.&nbsp;&nbsp; Choosing the right speakers for the situation&nbsp; will help reduce the chances of feedback. If&nbsp;&nbsp; you want some help choosing the best speakers,&nbsp; check out the free speaker specifications guide&nbsp;&nbsp; at audiouniversityonline.com/speaker-specs-guide/.&nbsp; Hit the "Like" button if you found this&nbsp;&nbsp; video helpful and subscribe to Audio&nbsp; University for more content like this.

  10. @smartalex2578: Here are 3 tips. First, throw every other vocal mic on the front line out of phase. Instead of anomalies caused by multiple mics loading up, they will now cancel out. This stabilizes both mains and monitors. Second, put about 14 ms delay on the mains. Not only does this align the sound from the back line with the mains, it simulates the mains being further away from the mics reducing the chance of feedback. Third, when ringing out monitors by driving them in to feedback, using a compressor limiter at the end of the chain with the threshold set very low to limit the volume of the feed back. This will  not only protect your ears, it will protect all of your speakers. You can now allow the speakers to feedback at a very minute level, giving you total immunity and allowing you to figure out what frequencies are feeding back assuming you don’t have an rta . Remember to return the threshold to a normal setting.  Btw, after setting the system up, during soundcheck drop the threshold of your limiters on both mains and monitors so it hits 3 db of gain reduction on peaks.  This protects your monitors as well as your ears, and helps to keep the musicians from getting audio fatigue.  - 40 years btw of dealing with feedback and musicians. - Misophonia, Phonophobia, and Hyperacusis: Auditory Sensitivity and Mental Health: Kind: captions Language: en The other day I got an email from a follower, and&nbsp; here's what it said: "I have been in and out of&nbsp;&nbsp; therapy for many, many years, mostly trying&nbsp; to find someone to help me with anxiety and&nbsp;&nbsp; depression related to environmental noise,&nbsp; which usually came from neighbors living in&nbsp;&nbsp; surrounding apartments, but no one has been able&nbsp; to give me any skills to get some relief from all&nbsp;&nbsp; this anxiety. And yes, my whole body does want to&nbsp; just avoid the sound because it feels horrible,&nbsp;&nbsp; more horrible than anything. So after decades&nbsp; of that apartment, living in turmoil, I finally&nbsp;&nbsp; moved to a house in a state park. The house was&nbsp; quiet for a year except for the leaf blowers,&nbsp;&nbsp; mostly in the autumn, but the last two months the&nbsp; airport must have redirected their flights to go&nbsp;&nbsp; right over my house every 15 minutes or so. So&nbsp; finally, living in my own house and in nature has&nbsp;&nbsp; become stressful, and even taking hikes in the&nbsp; woods have become very stressful for me to the&nbsp;&nbsp; point where I don't take care of myself anymore&nbsp; except for the basics. I don't know what to do.&nbsp;&nbsp; Do you have any suggestions? Okay. So first,&nbsp; I've got to say I usually don't have time to&nbsp;&nbsp; reply to all the emails and questions I get, and&nbsp; it's unethical for me to diagnose or recommend&nbsp;&nbsp; treatment to anyone over email. So here's what&nbsp; I told him. The first thing I recommended to him&nbsp;&nbsp; was that he worked with some informed provider&nbsp; to get a really good diagnosis of what's going&nbsp;&nbsp; on because it doesn't sound as much like an&nbsp; anxiety disorder to me as a sensory sensitivity&nbsp;&nbsp; or a hearing disorder. But then I got really&nbsp; curious because I don't know that much about sound&nbsp;&nbsp; sensitivities. So me being me, I spent the last&nbsp; month researching it and talking with experts,&nbsp;&nbsp; and here's what I've learned. There are at least&nbsp; three different types of sound sensitivities. And&nbsp;&nbsp; this matters because sound sensitivities sometimes&nbsp; co-occur with other mental health conditions&nbsp;&nbsp; like OCD, autism, anxiety, trauma, and sensory&nbsp; processing disorders. So let's talk about what&nbsp;&nbsp; the three conditions are, and then you'll learn a&nbsp; little bit about what the treatment options are. So there are three different types of sound&nbsp; sensitivities that we'll talk about today.&nbsp;&nbsp; If you get really angry about certain noises,&nbsp; that's misophonia. If you get really anxious or&nbsp;&nbsp; scared around certain sounds, that's phonophobia.&nbsp; And if everyday normal sounds are painfully loud,&nbsp;&nbsp; that's hyperacusis. So let's start with&nbsp; hyperacusis. Hyperacusis is when you perceive&nbsp;&nbsp; sounds as being louder than they objectively are.&nbsp; Everyday sounds seem louder than they should. Now,&nbsp;&nbsp; this can be caused by physical damage to&nbsp; the ears from loud noises or head injuries&nbsp;&nbsp; or acoustic trauma, but it's also associated&nbsp; with a ton of other physical conditions. 47%&nbsp;&nbsp; of people with hyperacusis also have anxiety. And&nbsp; while it does have overlap with the other forms&nbsp;&nbsp; of noise sensitivity, hyperacusis can sometimes be&nbsp; diagnosed as physically occurring within the ears.&nbsp;&nbsp; But because of the interplay of the brain's&nbsp; auditory processing system and how the brain&nbsp;&nbsp; interprets noise, hyperacuity can also be more of&nbsp; an issue with the nervous system hyperarousal. So&nbsp;&nbsp; it's essentially the brain perceiving loud noises&nbsp; to be a threat. Now, auditory sensitivity can&nbsp;&nbsp; occur on a spectrum where hyperacuity can be more&nbsp; on the more severe end of the spectrum and, like,&nbsp;&nbsp; a dislike of loud noises can be more on the less&nbsp; severe side of the spectrum. Auditory sensitivity&nbsp;&nbsp; can also be associated with sensory differences&nbsp; like um that often occur with autism, and it&nbsp;&nbsp; can stand alone. It can be that you just have&nbsp; really sensitive ears. Okay. Now let's talk about&nbsp;&nbsp; misophonia. Misophonia is when specific sounds&nbsp; trigger an, an intense reaction, usually anger,&nbsp;&nbsp; but it can also be irritation, rage, anxiety, and&nbsp; disgust. The most common triggers are repetitive&nbsp;&nbsp; sounds, like chewing, breathing, lip smacking, or&nbsp; tapping. Essentially, these sounds are triggering&nbsp;&nbsp; the nervous system reaction of fight in the&nbsp; fight/flight/freeze response, and this can lead to&nbsp;&nbsp; people to react with hostility or avoidance. Now,&nbsp; like hyperacusis, misophonia is not a recognized&nbsp;&nbsp; disorder in the DSM-5, and research is just&nbsp; beginning to understand the causes and treatment.&nbsp;&nbsp; There are some early studies indicating that there&nbsp; are brain differences in people with misophonia&nbsp;&nbsp; and that there's heightened response to sounds in&nbsp; the brain. Misophonia commonly occurs with other&nbsp;&nbsp; disorders, including anxiety, obsessive-compulsive&nbsp; personality disorder, autism spectrum disorder,&nbsp;&nbsp; and sensory processing disorder. There's also&nbsp; a higher incidence in people with tinnitus and&nbsp;&nbsp; hyperacusis. The co-occurrences may be better&nbsp; explained by symptom overlap. So for example,&nbsp;&nbsp; "Oh, that sound makes me anxious, but when&nbsp; I'm anxious that sound bothers me more." But&nbsp;&nbsp; the fact that the sound bothers you is not just&nbsp; anxiety. Okay. Now, let's talk about phonophobia.&nbsp;&nbsp; Phonophobia is an intense fear reaction&nbsp; to certain sounds. So like other phobias,&nbsp;&nbsp; it can be connected to anxiety disorders or a&nbsp; history of trauma, but it can also stand alone.&nbsp;&nbsp; One case study I read about was a girl who was&nbsp; 12, and after Chinese New Year, where she heard&nbsp;&nbsp; a lot of loud fireworks go off, she developed an&nbsp; acute electrifying intensified noise sensation in&nbsp;&nbsp; both ears when hearing sudden loud noises. And&nbsp; since then she began experiencing abnormally&nbsp;&nbsp; intensified sounds followed by unpleasant&nbsp; buzzing noises every time she was exposed&nbsp;&nbsp; to normal intensity sounds. So for example, the&nbsp; sound of a balloon popping or the rustle of a&nbsp;&nbsp; plastic bag were almost unbearable to her, to the&nbsp; point where she developed palpitations, shivering,&nbsp;&nbsp; excessive sweating, and crying. Her condition&nbsp; gradually worsened until she only wanted to spend&nbsp;&nbsp; time in a quiet room and was no longer attending&nbsp; social functions in school. So her parents took&nbsp;&nbsp; her to a doctor, and the ENT assessed her ears&nbsp; and her hearing and they found nothing wrong,&nbsp;&nbsp; nothing different about her hearing. They did&nbsp; an MRI and they found nothing wrong there,&nbsp;&nbsp; and then she got a thorough psychiatric evaluation&nbsp; and she was diagnosed with phonophobia. Now,&nbsp;&nbsp; phonophobia is generally considered an anxiety&nbsp; disorder, not a disorder in hearing. It's an&nbsp;&nbsp; intense fear of loud sounds. So for someone with&nbsp; a phonophobia, watching someone blow up a balloon&nbsp;&nbsp; until it bursts could possibly trigger a panic&nbsp; attack. Now, for the average person, loud sounds&nbsp;&nbsp; might be uncomfortable or anxiety-inducing, but&nbsp; with phonophobia it's extremely anxiety producing.&nbsp;&nbsp; Okay. So now let's talk about treatment. Right?&nbsp; With all three of these types of disorders,&nbsp;&nbsp; there are two main approaches to treating these&nbsp; noise sensitivities. So first I'm going to&nbsp;&nbsp; encourage you all to work with an audiologist&nbsp; to get a good diagnosis. An audiologist will&nbsp;&nbsp; rule out other conditions, and an audiologist&nbsp; who specializes in sound sensitivities can&nbsp;&nbsp; provide acoustic therapy. Okay. The second part of&nbsp; treatment is psychological. So counseling can help&nbsp;&nbsp; people decrease their anxiety response to sounds.&nbsp; You can learn to regulate your nervous-system&nbsp;&nbsp; response through grounding skills. But first,&nbsp; before we even go into that, we need to talk&nbsp;&nbsp; about how avoidance can make these conditions&nbsp; worse. Now, I want you to notice how, both with&nbsp;&nbsp; this girl from the case study and with the man who&nbsp; emailed me, they both had a drive to avoid noises,&nbsp;&nbsp; but as they did their symptoms worsened. So this&nbsp; this man who emailed me, he moves all the way out&nbsp;&nbsp; to a state park where it's really quiet. And for a&nbsp; year things were okay, but then gradually noises,&nbsp;&nbsp; even the quietest noises, like an airplane&nbsp; overhead, would bother him more and more. And and&nbsp;&nbsp; that's because sounds aren't just processed in the&nbsp; ears; they're processed in the brain. Our brain&nbsp;&nbsp; and our nervous systems are plastic. So that means&nbsp; that they adapt to the situations we experience.&nbsp;&nbsp; So when we experience a trigger like leaf blowers&nbsp; or a plastic bag rattling and then we avoid that&nbsp;&nbsp; trigger and we don't die, our brain thinks, "Whoo,&nbsp; I survived! Let's make my human avoid those things&nbsp;&nbsp; again because they must be dangerous." So your&nbsp; brain actually increases your anxiety response&nbsp;&nbsp; to those sounds. It's going to increase your&nbsp; sensitivity to those sounds. Avoiding triggers&nbsp;&nbsp; makes you more anxious. It's like if you're in&nbsp; a very dark room and you turn on a flashlight,&nbsp;&nbsp; it might feel uncomfortably bright. But if&nbsp; you're outdoors during the daytime and you turn&nbsp;&nbsp; on the exact same flashlight, it doesn't seem that&nbsp; bright. Your eyes adjusted so that situation isn't&nbsp;&nbsp; so uncomfortable. So with sound sensitivities your&nbsp; natural tendency is going to be to avoid or escape&nbsp;&nbsp; those sounds or to wear earplugs or headphones to&nbsp; block them out, but this will make things worse.&nbsp;&nbsp; So so now a regular sound is like a flashlight&nbsp; in a dark room; it hurts even more. We we can't&nbsp;&nbsp; completely eliminate sound. All we can do is&nbsp; develop greater courage in the face of these&nbsp;&nbsp; triggers. Right? So the first thing to remember&nbsp; is don't avoid or overprotect against sound,&nbsp;&nbsp; and then second is learn to change how you think&nbsp; about the sounds and the reactions you're having&nbsp;&nbsp; to them. So you've got to work on catastrophizing&nbsp; and blaming and labeling these triggers as bad.&nbsp;&nbsp; Um you gotta learn to accept sounds and refocus&nbsp; your attention and, you know, let go of self-pity&nbsp;&nbsp; and trying to change everyone else. So these&nbsp; are, you know, essential skills that, you know,&nbsp;&nbsp; in acceptance and commitment therapy, we we use&nbsp; the skills of willingness, acceptance, defusion,&nbsp;&nbsp; and understanding that the trigger, these noises&nbsp; are actually neutral, but our interpretations&nbsp;&nbsp; of it and our emotional response is what we can&nbsp; change, Now, let me put in a quick caveat because&nbsp;&nbsp; some therapists are going to teach coping skills&nbsp; and avoiding skills in response to these sounds,&nbsp;&nbsp; and that's actually going to make it worse.&nbsp; So that's why it's really important to find an&nbsp;&nbsp; informed provider. That's one of the reasons why I&nbsp; spent the last month researching this, and I still&nbsp;&nbsp; don't consider myself an expert in this in any&nbsp; way. I would definitely send someone with these&nbsp;&nbsp; disorders to a specialist. Okay. Then after you've&nbsp; started learning how to face these sounds and how&nbsp;&nbsp; to think differently about them, and perhaps&nbsp; you've done some acoustic therapy, we're gonna&nbsp;&nbsp; talk about how to soothe your nervous system. So&nbsp; if you can learn skills to calm yourself down,&nbsp;&nbsp; this will retrain your nervous system to&nbsp; not react to these triggering sounds in&nbsp;&nbsp; the same way. So some of these nervous&nbsp; system skills include deep breathing,&nbsp;&nbsp; the yawn, and progressive muscle relaxation.&nbsp; Now, I teach about 15 of these in my Grounding&nbsp;&nbsp; Skills for Anxiety course. It's free. So you can&nbsp; check that out. The link is in the description.&nbsp;&nbsp; But there's a lot of other ways to learn these&nbsp; grounding skills, and I've got also a bunch of&nbsp;&nbsp; these videos on the um on my YouTube channel,&nbsp; so check out my grounding skills playlist too.&nbsp;&nbsp; Okay. So after you have stopped avoiding&nbsp; these sounds, you um have worked on how&nbsp;&nbsp; you think about these sounds a little bit,&nbsp; and you've learned some grounding skills,&nbsp;&nbsp; now the next step is usually super-duper gentle,&nbsp; systematic exposure. This means that you're going&nbsp;&nbsp; to super gently face uncomfortable sounds. This&nbsp; literally rewires your brain to realize that these&nbsp;&nbsp; sounds aren't a threat. It's really important&nbsp; to know that you don't just expose yourself or&nbsp;&nbsp; a child to an uncomfortable sound just to get&nbsp; the child used to it. Right? This can actually&nbsp;&nbsp; make sound sensitivities much worse. Now, let's&nbsp; listen to Dr. Lindsey Tubaugh, who is a specialist&nbsp;&nbsp; in sound sensitivities, describe how she would&nbsp; use um acoustic therapy to treat one of these&nbsp;&nbsp; sound sensitivities. Sound therapy for any of the&nbsp; types of hyperacusis should really include a form&nbsp;&nbsp; of passive sound therapy, which is enriching&nbsp; your environment with soft, pleasant sound for&nbsp;&nbsp; as much of the day and night as possible. We&nbsp; don't want it loud. We don't want to hurt your&nbsp;&nbsp; ears. And if you need to take a listening break,&nbsp; that's great. But that's passive sound therapy.&nbsp;&nbsp; The idea is that it's a sound that your brain&nbsp; learns to tune out. Okay. And then active sound&nbsp;&nbsp; therapy. Active sound therapy is using that window&nbsp; of tolerance for 20 minutes a day. This is really&nbsp;&nbsp; for misophonia. This is what active sound therapy&nbsp; looks like. A lot of times we start with the the&nbsp;&nbsp; triggering sound. I'll either create it for them&nbsp; myself or I'll have a loved one create it for&nbsp;&nbsp; them. So you're doing, you're doing something&nbsp; fun for 20 minutes. Every five minutes you&nbsp;&nbsp; turn it on, turn it off. And typically that's not&nbsp; long enough for people to trigger. You can start&nbsp;&nbsp; with a three -econd exposure and then get back to&nbsp; another five minutes of doing what you love. So&nbsp;&nbsp; basically we're exposing that person to that sound&nbsp; without having that trigger response. Okay. Next,&nbsp;&nbsp; you know, just in general when you're treating&nbsp; these things you want to decrease your overall&nbsp;&nbsp; stress and increase, increase your overall health,&nbsp; and that's going to improve your resilience. And&nbsp;&nbsp; then of course, as always, like I said, you want&nbsp; to get professional support - an audiologist,&nbsp;&nbsp; an ENT, and a trained mental health professional&nbsp; who can help with these co-occurring conditions.&nbsp;&nbsp; Okay. So let's go back to this 12-year-old girl.&nbsp; She and her parents, this is how treatment went&nbsp;&nbsp; for them. They had two therapy sessions where they&nbsp; learned all about phonophobia and its treatment.&nbsp;&nbsp; Right? Just psycho-education. And then the&nbsp; therapist taught the girl how to calm her nervous&nbsp;&nbsp; system using deep breathing and progressive muscle&nbsp; relaxation. Then after she mastered those skills,&nbsp;&nbsp; they started in with gradual exposure. So they&nbsp; start with the least provoking stimulus at first,&nbsp;&nbsp; like drawing a smiling balloon and then drawing&nbsp; a bursting balloon. Right? So I, just to be super&nbsp;&nbsp; clear, they're not just taking this girl and&nbsp; popping balloons around her all the time. Right?&nbsp;&nbsp; Super gradual. Just drawing pictures of balloons.&nbsp; Okay. After the child was comfortable with this,&nbsp;&nbsp; the stimulus was then increased to bursting&nbsp; inflated balloons in the clinic and at home&nbsp;&nbsp; with parents. The parents acted as a co-therapist&nbsp; in the situation. And each successful session was&nbsp;&nbsp; rewarded accordingly. The child showed tremendous&nbsp; improvement in her symptoms after three months&nbsp;&nbsp; of therapy. And then gradually the child was&nbsp; brought to public places, like a restaurant,&nbsp;&nbsp; and finally it was brought again, we watch a&nbsp; fireworks show with no resulting complications&nbsp;&nbsp; after six months of therapy. Okay. So going back&nbsp; to the man who emailed me. Again, I recommended&nbsp;&nbsp; that he visit an audiologist and a mental health&nbsp; professional who specialize in these conditions to&nbsp;&nbsp; get a good diagnosis and treatment. And you can&nbsp; too. With the right support and education you&nbsp;&nbsp; really can learn the skills to manage and decrease&nbsp; sound sensitivities like hyperacusis, misophonia,&nbsp;&nbsp; and phonophobia. Okay. Your lens choice when shooting a video will&nbsp;&nbsp; cause the biggest change in how everything&nbsp; looks. WAY more than which camera you use,&nbsp;&nbsp; and maybe on par with what type of&nbsp; and what style of lighting you use. The&nbsp;&nbsp; same with photography - If youre setting up&nbsp; a portrait of someone and trying to design&nbsp;&nbsp; what the overall photo will look like. The&nbsp; difference is SO massive when choosing a lens. Same principal in the studio - if you move the&nbsp; camera back while zooming in the lens, you get&nbsp;&nbsp; the same subject, but the background zooms in. Use&nbsp; this to shape the finished shot to your liking.

The downside is the plastic mount feels a bit flimsy, like it might just give up on life after a few too many passionate gaming moments. Would I buy it? But let's be real, it's a sensitive old man, picking up every little background noise like your keyboard clacking or your neighbor's dog having an existential crisis. Because 32-bit float audio won't really ever peak. So, there are three main types of microphones: dynamic, condenser, and ribbon microphones. But for today's purposes, we're going to skip over ribbon mics. Every microphone you saw at the beginning of this video was either a condenser or a dynamic mic. Now, dynamic and condenser microphones actually operate differently. They have different mechanisms built on the inside. So, naturally, they're used for different scenarios. Dynamic mics have a coil on the inside that moves based on changing sound pressure, while condenser mics have a small membrane stretched across the plate. But what's the main difference between the two mechanisms? Well, dynamic mics are super reliable and rugged. Microphones like the SH SM57 and the SH SM7B are great examples and are incredible options. These microphones have incredible sound rejection from the back, which gives you an advantage if you're recording in a noisy environment. They also have a bass presence feature, which means the closer you get to the microphone, the more bass will be present. This is why so many people opt to use this microphone for YouTube or podcast applications because it gives your voice that kind of booming presence. Now, condenser microphones tend to be a lot more sensitive than dynamic mics. They are really good for picking up a lot of fine detail, which is great if you have a nice recording environment. They also tend to be a little bit more fragile, so you kind of have to handle these guys with a little more care. Okay, so now you know the main difference between dynamic and condenser mics. Let's move on to step two. So, what do you plan to use your microphone for? Are you making YouTube videos, recording songs from your bedroom, or are you the guy in the friend group that keeps saying we should start a podcast? Guys, now I can't claim that these guidelines are going to work for everybody, but here are my general recommendations. Recording artist, if you're making beats in your bedroom or any room without soundproofing, get a dynamic mic. If you feel good about your sound treatment, then go for a condenser. YouTubers, get either a lapel mic with a receiver that can attach to your camera or a dynamic mic that can stay at your desk for local recording. and podcasters, just buy a Sure SM7B. You won't have to replace the mic. It's exactly what you're looking for. Just get the freaking microphone. [Music] Now, every microphone also has what is called a polar pattern. A polar pattern is simply just a graph showing where the microphone is more sensitive. Here are some of the most common patterns. The outline areas show where the sound will be picked up when recording. - HOW TO FILM IN A SMALL SPACE - YouTube Setup For Beginners - Filmmaking 101: Kind: captions Language: en if you're trying to film your videos in a room the size of a large closet this video is for you three two one hi and welcome or welcome back to diy film with merle becker the channel where i help you make better videos i've got four great tips for you with regards to shooting in small spaces and i've got a bonus tip for you at the end of this video so be sure to stick around so many of you are shooting youtube videos or maybe films in your apartment or your house maybe you're doing makeup tutorials in your bathroom or maybe you're lucky enough to have an extra room that you can set up as a youtube studio but in many cases your room is pretty making your shots look great in a small room is challenging why well quite often shots in smaller spaces end up looking flat and sometimes relatively boring and the most common reason is because in a small space it's hard to add depth to your shot and shots with depth are generally more interesting so your goal in a smaller space is to add depth or create a setup that has a foreground middle ground and background so with this in mind here are four easy tips you can use for shooting in small spaces number one film in the corner when film directors are setting up their shots they're always thinking about how they can create depth because as mentioned shots with depth are more interesting so they often do things like shoot with a low f-stop so the background is blurry and the subject is in focus or they try to move their subject far away from the wall so again for the same reason but when you're in a small room you can't move your subject far from the wall because there's no room to do that but one thing you can do is shift your setup so that you have the corner of the room in the background instead of the wall giving you a little more space between the background and your subject having the corner in your background not only creates a little more depth but it also creates leading lines that draw attention to your subject again making the shot more interesting for those of you who don't know what leading lines are they're basically lines in your shot that point to your subject drawing the eye to your subject and for more unleading lines and other ways to frame your shots to create interest check out my other video on framing your shots i'll leave a link in the description so shift everything to the corner of your room and let the added distance and the leading lines add interest to your shot number two put small objects in the so say the corner trick doesn't work for you for whatever reason one other thing you can do to add depth is to add a few small objects in your choose objects that either relate to your video or your scene and make sure they're not too large or distracting and to place them either on the left or the right of the frame because by putting little objects in your foreground you're again adding depth you're making it look more like there's a distinct background subject and foreground in the shot so try a few different objects out until you find the right one and the right placement and see how this little trick instantly makes your shot more interesting number three add a backlight one of the most common ways to add depth to a scene is with lighting most of you are already using one light to light your subject's face but did you know that if you placed another light behind and above your subject you can create a little more depth you see when you place the other light called a backlight back here it makes this little rim of light on your subject's head it's like a little shiny halo on their hair and this halo gives a little more separation between the subject and your background to do this you can place your backlight on something like a bookshelf or maybe attach it to a pipe already in your room or i recommend picking up a relatively cheap light and stand from amazon and placing the stand to the right or left of your subject just out of the shot i'll leave a link to a cheap light you can use in the description just make sure your light is high enough over your subject's head so it's shining down on them and don't forget to check your framing to make sure the light itself isn't in your shot so add a backlight to your small room setup today to add depth interest and to make your subject really pop number four use colored lights in the one of the easiest ways to make a boring flat wall more interesting is to add a splash of color and one of the most dynamic ways to do this is with light in fact you see this trick all over youtube's creators often use colored lights on their small set to make it more interesting especially in smaller sometimes they use strings of colored bulbs sometimes i use colored lights shining on the wall behind them as for me i use a little luma cube in the back of my room which i like because it can dial in pretty much any color i'll put a link in the description so when placing your light by the wall put it relatively low and face your light towards the wall so that the color reflects off the wall and when you're choosing your color try to pick a color that somehow relates to your content or other elements on your set or better yet choose a color that complements the other things in your room so add some interest by sticking colored lights in the background and if you're at a loss for what color to choose pick one that relates to your theme and pretty soon your audience will forget that you're shooting in the closet alright so let's recap if you're shooting in a small room four ways you can make it more interesting are to film in a corner put objects in the foreground add a backlight and use colored lights in the background alright let's do a quick tip for those of you who don't have a dedicated youtube shooting room one of the most time consuming elements of your shoot is the setup but one quick way to cut down on that time is to first set up your shot so you love it then mark where everything goes with painters tape this means your light positions your camera position your props etc just stick little pieces of tape where each item goes and maybe write the item name on it if you need to then when it comes time to set up all you have to do is put everything back on the pieces of tape and there's no time wasted fiddling around trying to find the perfect shot you may have to look at some tape during the week but most would agree the time saved makes it worth it okay so go tackle that small space and make it shine and if you found any of this helpful please give the video a thumbs up subscribe and hit the bell so you know when the next one is posted and i will catch you next time so you

- 5 Simple Tips for Better Talking Head Videos (Beginner Guide): Kind: captions Language: en A lot of people think their talking head videos would be better if they just had a shiny new camera. But I can tell you from experience that's not the case. In this video, I'm going to share five simple tips you can implement without buying any new gear that can instantly level up your talking head videos. And to drive this point home, I'm actually filming this video on my iPhone and using a budget lavalier microphone. The first tip is to fix your ey line, which is where your camera sits in relation to your eye level. If the camera's too low, you end up looking down into it and that can feel awkward and disengaged. Or if the camera's too high and you're looking up, that can feel really stiff or unnatural. What you want is your camera just slightly above eye level. Not way up, just enough that you're looking straight into the lens and not down at it. A simple trick is to raise your camera a few more inches than really feels normal. Record a short clip and then watch it back. Most people are surprised at how good it looks. And this simple trick alone can make your videos feel more confident and more intentional. The next thing to fix is lighting. And this is where beginners can really over complicate things. You don't need multiple lights. You don't need fancy colors. And you don't have to light up the entire room. The reality is one soft light pointed off to the side at about a 45° angle will almost always serve you better than multiple harsh lights pointed straight at you. The thing to remember is it's not about brightness. It's actually about softness. You want a gentle light that wraps around your face and not harsh shadows or blown out highlights. A good rule of thumb is your face should be the brightest thing in the frame. If the background is competing for attention, the lighting is actually working against you. Keep it simple. The reality is one soft light aimed well is going to take you way farther than multiple lights stacked on top of each other just because you think you're supposed to. The next thing to fix is your audio and specifically how close your microphone is. Many beginners, just like I did, keep the microphone too far away because, frankly, it feels awkward having it up close. But this is actually the fastest way to make your audio sound thin, echoey, and amateur. A simple rule is your microphone should be closer than really feels normal. Whether it's a desk mic, a shotgun mic, or a lavalier mic, getting the mic closer to your mouth is fuller, richer, and more professional. The reality is that clean audio matters more than good video. Viewers can forgive the bad lighting or a messy background, but if they struggle to hear you, they're gone. So, before you buy a new camera or tweak your lighting again, just move the microphone closer, record a test clip, and watch it back. Most of the time, this one change makes a bigger difference than you might think. The next thing to focus on is your delivery and specifically how fast you're talking. Almost everyone talks faster on camera than they actually realize, especially when they're nervous or trying to get everything right. The problem is talking faster makes the message harder to follow and it actually makes you sound less confident than you actually are. A simple fix is to slow down by about 10 to 15%. This usually means pausing a little bit between thoughts and letting a sense finish before jumping to the next one. And the weird part is that when you're recording, it's actually going to feel too slow, but then when you watch it back, most of the time it sounds clearer and more confident. And if you're not sure, record two test clips, one at your normal pace and one at a slightly slower pace, and just compare them. I think most people will prefer the slower version. The last tip is about how you record, and this one makes everything easier. Don't try to record your video in one perfect take. That's a lot of pressure and almost guarantees to make your delivery worse. Instead, break it up into short sections. Finish a section, pause, reset, and then move on to the next one. And if you mess up, you only have to redo that one section. And this does two things. First, it helps you stay relaxed and sound more natural. And second, it makes editing faster and easier because you're not digging through one absurdly long clip trying to find some good parts. The viewers don't expect perfection, but they do expect clarity. Recording in short sections means you only have to focus on saying one thing well at a time, and that's really how better talking head videos get made. So, if you focus on these five things, you don't need better gear. You just need better habits. Simple adjustments like camera height, simple lighting, cleaner audio, slower delivery, and recording in sections really adds up fast. And once those are dialed in, everything else gets easier. If you're just getting started, don't overthink it. Pick one of these tips, apply it to your next video, and then step up from there. That's exactly how you get better over time. Let me know in the comments if any of these tips resonated with you. And if you like this video, please consider subscribing for more content like this. And you can click here for more videos to help on your content creation journey.

- Hiding a Mic in Plain Sight: Kind: captions Language: en Hey indie filmmakers—Griffin here. Today I want to make this shot sound better. I’m hearing too much room right now, because it’s a really wide shot and my mic has to be farther away than I’d like. Of course I could get much closer with a lav mic, but I don’t want to see this in the shot, and I don’t always like the sound I get with a lav. It can be really bassy against my chest, and it can rub against clothing. Let’s bring the mic into the shot, all the way down into the shot. Now I sound much clearer! So today I’ll show you how easy it is to cover this thing up, but still get all the benefits from a close, invisible mic. Most of the time, I shoot interviews pretty close, and it’s easy to keep the mic just below the frame. But as you can see in this interview from my film Sriracha, in a wider shot, sometimes I mess it up. Nick: Back up a little bit for me. Nick: Back the mic out a little bit for me. Luckily, most of my interviews are on tripod, and all I have to do is find one clean part of the shot, mask that on top, and no one knows how often I make mistakes. Here’s how we can get rid of this offending microphone in Final Cut Pro X. I'll start by holding option and dragging this clip. That quickly creates a duplicate. I'll lower the audio on this extra one. And then, I'll bring up the T tool. That's the Trim tool. And if I drag across the clip, it'll change the in and out points at the same time, and I can find a part of the clip that doesn't have a microphone in it. Just a clean plate—this part's perfect. Now if I go to Effects, under Masks, and drag Draw Mask onto this clip, I can now draw an area just around the microphone. So I'll disable this clip with V first, just so I can see the microphone again. And now I can draw around it. I'll set some control points here. And now when I re-enable this clip, it hides the microphone. And I could add some feathering, just to make sure that no one will ever see this mask. Five years ago, I shot a commercial with a lot of chicken noise in the background, so this is the first time I realized I could put a mic blatantly in the shot. As long as none of these chickens fly up into the top part of the frame, masking out the mic is easy. I’m still using that technique today. When I interviewed photographer Ben Grunow in Switzerland, I placed myself right in the locked down shot, so I could control his eyeline. I wanted his eyes looking near the camera, and I’d also love the best audio possible. Just be careful that hands don’t move behind this invisible microphone, or anything else moving in the background. So you know the drill: Find a clean frame, draw a mask, and cover the secret microphone. It may feel like cheating, but don’t feel bad about it. Hollywood has a tradition of hiding mics in plain sight. "There’s a microphone right there in the bush. You have to talk into it.” Thanks for watching, and if you’re curious about the mics that I’m using, you can go to griffinhammond.com/gear for a full list of the equipment that I use, including this Rode NTG-3 microphone. And if you missed it last week, here’s a link to watch my review of the SpiderLight holster. [to Andy the cat] Hey friend!

I will also add a few tutorials about how I use them and how to position them correctly. Everything you need to know. Now you have the camera, the lens, the lights. And if you learn how to use them, this is the final result. But we need to talk about the audio because this is more important than the video. And after I will give you a bonus tip because I want you to shoot easier and faster. I will demonstrate one thing fast. The audio you hear now is recorded with the AT875R microphone from Audio Technica and the Scarlet Solo fourth generation audio interface. Many beginner creators say that recording audio separately, it's so complicated and it's better to record in camera using a wireless lav mic. Yes, you can save 30 seconds or a few minutes, but all the wireless lav mics, at least affordable ones, have something in common. A lav mic is omnidirectional. Yes, those roads, DJI, Holyland are omnidirectional. That means it will pick up everything around them. If you shoot outdoors and you have a big distance between you and the camera, those are great. But in a normal apartment room, acoustically untreated, we have a lot of reverb and that will sound bad. This is the sound recorded with the shotgun microphone. One, two, three. Sound test. One, two, three. And this is the sound recorded with a professional expensive wireless lav mic straight in the camera. And this is an expensive one. Because we shoot at home in a studio, we can build a better setup. Trust me, the quality difference is big and after a few recordings, you will get used to it, but the quality will be much higher. With the 8875R, you can record like this, having it close to your mouth, and the result is incredible. Or if you want it outside of the shot, you can do it. This is an $189 microphone connected to the Scarlet Solo 4 generation, a $139 audio interface. I use a mic cable that costs $10 and the mic arm that's $70. The Solo has one preamp and if you need two mics for podcasting, you can choose the 2i2 version with two preamps. I record the audio in Da Vinci Resolve where I also edit the videos. And when I finish the recording, I just insert the video on the timeline. Select both, right click, choose auto align clips, choose waveform, and click sync. That's it. I select them again, right click, and click on link to keep them locked and mute the audio recorded in camera. Now I have the audio synchronized, but this great audio, come on, it was fast. Of course, any microphone, cheap or expensive, wired or wireless, requires some audio processing according to your taste and recording conditions. But for that, I will also add a link in the description to see how I do it for inspiration. And if you shoot in various places in your apartment and don't have the PC near you to connect the audio interface, I still recommend the Zoom F3, an exceptional field recorder with 32-bit float, which is excellent. Now you have the camera and the lens, the lights, and the audio recording setup. We can add one final touch that will make your creation process much easier and this is the Elgato prompter. If you script your videos to be more articulate and concise with your words, this is a musth have. measured on a scale of 0 to 100 and ideally you want the light that you purchase to be at 95 and up for both specs thankfully nowadays most lights are at or above these requirements after that we have the wattage in light output wattage is a measurement of the total amount of power that the light will draw as it runs and typically speaking the higher the wattage the more output in light intensity that the light will have output is measured in Lux at a certain distance usually 1 M so for an example 4,000 Lux at 1 meter it's going to be more than enough for YouTube videos like this or talking head interviews 30,000 Lux at 1 meter is very bright enough to fully illuminate a very big space and then 90,000 Lux at 1 meter is enough to wake pick up a demon from the underworld this is what you'd use to essentially mimic the sun in an outdoor shoot where the natural light from the sun had already faded now speaking of power draw the next thing we need to talk about is the ability to work off of a battery if you're always filming indoors next to an outlet it's not really a huge concern for you but if you're ever going to be in a situation where you're away from power outlets it would make sense to have a light that can work off of a battery some lights have internal batteries built in that'll work for a couple hours other have the ability to work off of Industry standard batteries like Sony NP or vmount batteries this is nice cuz you can stack up a ton of them and make sure that the light will work for hours up next you're going to want to consider if you need special lighting - Watch this BEFORE you buy a Camera!: Kind: captions Language: en In the last 15 years, I've used and tested over hundreds of cameras, and I've compiled down a list of the 10 most important things to look out for when shopping for one, so your hard-earned dollars will not go wasted on something that does not fit your needs. The Von's guide to buying cameras, and I'll also be including some of my personal recommendations at the end of the video. Sensor size. So, the first thing I would look out for is the size of the camera sensor, and it's usually the first thing listed on the website description. For example, the Sony ZV10 on b&H.com. Right under key features, 24.2 megapixel APS-C sensor. The Canon PowersShot G7X 20.2 megapixel 1 in stack CMOS sensor. Now, sensor sizes matter because it's what's going to really help us level up from the photos that we can take from our phones. And since most phones use a sensor size smaller than 1 in, obviously, we're going to want something larger than 1 in. But without boring you too much with the differences between all these different sensor sizes, I recommend cameras with at least an APS-C sensor. At this point here, we're definitely going to get better image quality over our phones, better lowlight performance, and that signature pro look, that beautiful background blur, aka authentic portrait mode. But the reason why I highly recommend APS-C cameras to beginners is because one, they tend to be lighter and smaller. two, a very affordable ecosystem, especially when it comes to shopping for lenses. And three, they're not just for beginners. APS-C is very much capable to produce pro results that can rival bigger sensors. And Fujifilm and Sony make some of the best mirrorless cameras in the market right now. And I'll have my personal recommendations for you at the end of the video. Mirrorless versus DSLR. Speaking of mirrorless, we need to update your lingo because loads of people are still calling every pro camera they see a DSLR. But actually, mirrorless cameras are not DSLRs. Again, without getting into the whole technical mumbo jumbo of things. In summary, DSLRs are old technology. They have fewer autofocus points. They are slower to focus. There's a lot of guess work involved. Plus, they're chunky and they're clunky. Mirrorless cameras, on the other hand, are new technology. They're sexy. They're way better at autofocusing. They even have human face and eye autofocus, which will make all of our lives so much easier. And any setting changes that we make on the camera, we'll see on our screen as we're making them. We call that realtime preview, which will make learning camera basics so much easier. And that's something that we can't do with DSLRs. And plus, mirrorless cameras are way better for video as well. Now, the only pro that I see with DSLRs is that you can get them for cheap. So, if you're doing strictly photography and want to save a ton of money, DSLRs and lenses made for DSLRs might be worth considering if you're willing to put up with the bigger learning curve. Saving money. Photography versus videography. So, like we were just saying, mirrorless cameras are better for photos and they're better for videos. However, depending on how much and how important video is to you, this is an area where we can actually save some extra money. So, let me break this down into three separate tiers, and this will make a lot of sense to you by the end of the section. Mirrorless cameras that are capable of shooting max 1080p full HD would be the cheapest. And there's nothing wrong with 1080p videos. They are still being widely viewed to this day, especially on social media and on your phone. So, you don't really need 4K. But if you want 4K, cameras that are capable of shooting max 4K 30 frames per second are actually coming down in price. However, cameras that are capable of shooting 4K, 60 frames per second, 120 frames per second, and even beyond that, like 8K, they're expensive, and it's these higher video specs that drive up the prices of modern-day mirrorless cameras. However, if you're doing strictly photos, you can largely ignore these types of cameras. You can get an older mirrorless camera that came out 10 years ago for cheap, like this Sony a6000 right here, and the results will still be just as good as the photos from cameras that came out today. As long as the camera can shoot raw photos, you actually have a lot of powers in your hands to edit them into stunning images. Besides that, I would argue as long as the camera has human face detection autofocus, it's going to be good enough. Phase versus contrast detection autofocus. Now, speaking of autofocus, there's something that we need to look out for in the description. What kind of autofocus system is the camera using? And I would say this is one of my biggest deal breakers. Not to be confused with face autofocus, you want to look out for something called phase detection autofocus. Basically, this is what enables the camera to have that snappy and fast autofocus, especially when you're tracking a moving subject. You want to have this, especially when it comes to video. You want the camera to focus immediately on you or your subject. What you don't want is contraston detection. It's just slow AF in both meanings. It's kind of like us waking up without our morning coffee. We can still function, but just very slowly. Now, most mirrorless cameras up to 10 years back will likely have both, so it's not going to be a huge problem. But there will be one or two models that only have contrast detection autofocus. And that's something that you need to be wary of. Make sure it has phase detection autofocus. You're talking about my face. Inbody image stabilization. My second biggest deal breakaker. A camera that I'm shopping for should always have inbody image stabilization or IBIS for short, not the hotel. It is this mechanism inside the camera that helps compensate for shakes when you're handh holding it to ensure that you get sharp results. And it's usually measured by stops. Five is usually typical. Anything more than that is pretty amazing. Now, with that said, having IBIS is not entirely necessary if you're doing strictly photos. Utilizing a faster shutter speed will ensure you sharp results. It's when you're shooting videos that IBIS becomes a necessity. It is a night and day difference having it versus not having it. And the frustrating thing is there are still modern-day mirrorless cameras that are coming out that does not have built-in image stabilization. - Stop Using Video Lights Like This! (5 Common Lighting Mistakes): Kind: captions Language: en - You might be using your lights all wrong and so here are the five most common lighting mistakes. Number one is lighting positioning. A lot of people put their lights at eye level but you really want your camera at eye level and your lighting to be just above the camera. This is gonna help shape your face a bit better and add more dimension to the shot with extra shadows to give you a bit more definition in the face as well. And this isn't the only way to do it. This is just kind of the standard. So definitely experiment and see how this looks for you. But one of the things you can avoid by moving your lights up above the camera is those reflections in glasses. So definitely take this into consideration when you're placing your lights. Now you need to remember that every light has a set color temperature. Some are set to daylight, like this and some are warmer, like this. Now the GVM SD600D can be set to a daylight temperature and it can also be changed to a warmer temperature like this, which is called a bi color light. For most YouTube setups, I recommend setting your light to 5,600 kelvin, and this is gonna replicate daylight. So as you're outside, this is gonna give you that same type of color that the sun will give you in midday. Or if you're buying a light, the chances are it's already gonna be set to 5,600 kelvin, right around there or set to a daylight temperature. Now, the most common mistake that I see people making is that they're not white balancing their camera to their key light. Now the key light is the main light that you're using and you wanna make sure that your camera settings match the color temperature of that light. This is what it can look like when your white balance is off in your camera. And then this is what it can look like with the correct white balance. So don't blame your lights quite yet for making your skin color look all weird. Try setting the custom white balance in your camera first and we have a full tutorial on how to do this in the link in the description. Now you can use the cameras automatic white balance and that's gonna make it really easy for you but you do risk the colors slightly changing throughout your video when you're filming. And that's why I always recommend just setting the white balance manually inside your camera. The third mistake that I commonly see is not using diffusion on your lights and this is just gonna give you a really harsh lighting setup. Soft boxes are such an easy way to add a much more cinematic lighting to your shot. Here you can see what it looks like with no diffusion. And then here is what it looks like with a soft box added in. You can get soft boxes for almost any light, even these LED panels. You can pop these open and stick them on the LED panels. And these really help add a much softer light to these small light sources. But when it comes to using soft boxes, I really do prefer to use a COB or a cob light. These lights were really made to be used with soft boxes and adding this soft box is gonna make your lighting look so much better, but it's also gonna make your light a little bit darker, which is why cob lights are so awesome to use is because they get much brighter, especially for the price that you're paying. Keep in mind that these lights get very bright but they're intended to be used with soft boxes. So you definitely need the space in your room or in your setup to be using a light like this. On these cob lights, they have attachments made for these soft boxes so it's really easy to slide it in and snap in a soft box to that light. And on the SD600D light by GVM, you can use a large soft box and still get tons of light out of this because this light is so bright. If you're someone who is shooting videos outside or you need something that is just extremely bright because maybe you need to place it a bit farther away in a podcast setup or something like that, I think this is a great light for you to get. Now, there are lights similar to this like the Aputure 600D but those are going to be a lot more expensive. So especially if you can catch this on sale like it is right now, you can save a lot of money by going with this light and still have an incredibly bright and high quality light. Now, the fourth common mistake that I see a lot of people doing is using their lights at full brightness, especially when it's not needed. When you're using a bright light at full brightness, you have to darken the image in your camera and this is going to darken kind of the natural setting that you're in. And this makes the lighting look very harsh and kind of spotlighty. Now, one easy tip that you can do is start by turning your main key light off and then adjusting your camera until the setting around you looks good, and the background lights and the lamps that you have until those look good and then you're gonna bring your key light in next and then you can adjust that key light to your shot. Now, mistake number five that I commonly see people doing is using way too many lights. And truly, if you just have one soft box, you can get away with lighting your YouTube setup with just one light. If you click on the screen, I'll show you exactly how to place it and how to set it up to get a cinematic image.

- Albums that Changed Music: Talking Heads - Remain in Light: Kind: captions Language: en talking head's 1980 album remain in light took the band's conventional method of writing and recording songs and flipped it on its head it's an incredible album whose influence reaches far and wide and today we're talking all about the writing recording and the influences of this timeless album [Music] hello there it's warren hewitt here i hope you're doing marvelously well welcome back to another episode of the series if you haven't already please subscribe and if you hit the notification bell you'll be notified when we have a new video of course you can if you're into production go to producelikeapro.com and sign up for the email list and get a whole bunch of free goodies in january of 1980 talking heads were on an unofficial break they returned to new york city after touring to support their third album fear of music and decided to take some time off as a band frontman david byrne began working with producer brian eno on what would become a separate project my life in the bush of ghosts and keyboardist jerry harrison produced an album for soul singer nona hendrix at the sigma sound studios branch in new york meanwhile talking heads bassist and drummer married couple tina weymouth and chris franz considered leaving the band they felt that talking heads was becoming nothing more than a group to back up from and david byrne so unsure of what to do franz and weymouth headed to the caribbean for a two-week vacation to consider their options here they had the opportunity to practice native percussion instruments and meet the reggae rhythm duo sly and robbie after this trip concluded france and weymouth purchased an apartment above compass point studios in nassau in the bahamas this is the studio where the talking heads have recorded their original album more songs about buildings and food soon david byrne joined them followed by brian eno even though eno had worked with the band on their previous two albums he was reluctant to work with them again but upon hearing a demo tape of what they were working on he changed his mind talking heads and brian eno set out to write and record what would become the 1980 masterpiece remain in light on this album talking heads took a different approach in their songwriting instead of thinking of the group as a singer and a backup band the goal for this album was as burns described sacrificing our egos for mutual cooperation so instead of the ban writing music around david byrne's lyrics they instead decided to begin with instrumental jams building songs around the best parts talking heads were inspired by a more communal way of making music integrating polyrhythms from africa the 1973 record aphrodisiac by nigerian artist fella cutie was a particular inspiration talking heads began recording in july of 1980 at compass point studios brian edo played a critical role in this process as they began to blend african musical influences with rock encouraging experimentation along the way he compared this creative process to looking out to the world and saying what a fantastic place we live in let's celebrate it so the band began experimenting recording long extended jam sessions and singling out the best parts rather than focusing on chord progressions as you might do in rock talking heads kept it to a relatively simple musical form focusing instead on layers of poly rhythms these were the days before looping audio was a simple task that you could do with a computer so when they found a few bars they light they would single them out and then record themselves playing just those parts over and over keeping it simple so they could build the track from there david byrne described this process as turning themselves into human samplers this use of a longer simpler jam orientated song is something talking heads had experimented with on the song isimbra from the album fear of music but this was just the starting point eno had the band repeat this process over many times trying to get the band not to worry too hard about what they were playing as jerry harrison recalls we had gone down to the bahamas and was setting out to do an album that we hadn't attempted in the past we did this because we noticed in our rehearsals that sometimes the first time you play a song you do it with this instant sense of expiration and that's different than you'll ever play a song again we wanted to see if we could capture something like that so we laid down the parts one person at the studio at a time with the general mindset of oh i want to try it there were lots and lots of tracks we used the mixing board as the composition to move from part a to part b we were really on a roll we worked three weeks in the bahamas ac dc was in the next room doing back in black and we cut all of our basic tracks at the same amount of time they required for one guitar solo we were really in a groove right from the beginning of romanian light this combination of loops extended jams and poly rhythms is on full display let's take a listen to the opening track born under punches the heat goes on cool take a look at these while this unique creative process did create some incredible music it could also be frustrating at times engineer rhett davies left the project upset with a fast-paced environment eno was having them work in steven stanley who had previously worked with bob marley stepped in as engineer in his place talking heads were aware that the world was in the middle of a musical shift sugar hill gang's hit rapper's delight was released only a year before and hip-hop was on the rise talking head's own drummer chris franz had provided the backbeat on the curtis blows song the breaks and france had shown the song to david byrne on the song cross-eyed and painless burn could be heard emulating chris blow's percussive delivery break it up break it up break it up break down getting the best of them most of the instruments were recorded at compass point studios with additional recording taking place at sigma sound in new york city where harrison had previously worked berna nino began tweaking the songs that they recorded in the bahamas adding musicians like adrian baloo on guitar nota hendrix on backing vocals and trumpet player john hassell the song houses in motion features john hassell predominantly his unique avant-garde style perfectly matches the rest of the production on this album when it came to writing the lyrics david byrne was suffering from a bad case of writer's block he felt that these were incredibly unique songs and needed lyrics to match so he took an approach similar to how the band had recorded the instruments burn began to assemble lyrical phrases pulling inspiration from a variety of sources like news headlines radio preachers and even john dean's watergate testimony this lyrical technique can be seen in the album's best known song once in a lifetime this song was almost abandoned but after brian eno developed a wordless melody for the chorus david byrne wrote the words in the same manner as the rest of the album combining phrases from various sources for this song in particular he can be heard imitating the vocal delivery of a preacher rhythmically while the band felt beat one in one place brian enough felt it differently interpreting the third b as beat one this was something he encouraged to give a different feel to the song emphasizing some different rhythmic elements as eno remembers this means the song has a funny balance with two centers of gravity their funk groove and my dubby reggae-ish understanding of it a bit like the way felicity songs have multiple rhythms going on at the same time warping in and out of each other a lexicon 224 digital reverb effects unit was utilized on this song and album it was obtained by engineer and mixer dave jordan and it was one of the first of its kind once in a lifetime showcases this reverb unique rhythms and burns preacher-like vocal delivery it's all a great example of how brian eno encouraged musical experimentation in the studio as jerry harrison recalls eno had taught us to think of the studio as an extension of the instrument you had if you look back at the photos of the beatles engineers they were wearing white lab coats they were totally behind the glass you didn't go in there the musicians were in the other room and captured by the technicians maybe if you were lucky they would play the music back through the speakers eno broke that barrier down everything was an instrument it was all taking place when i described my keyboard wash in once in a lifetime it was a performance by me and eno in the control room being captured because we had a trusting relationship with him we were all cool with that and it also meant that we began to become familiar with all of the equipment in the control room this cut and dry roll between the musicians technicians and producers started to become more fluid now let's take a listen to all of these experiments combining on one timeless song once in a where is lifetime large automobile and you may tell yourself this is not my beautiful house and you may tell yourself this is not my beautiful wife same as it ever was same as it ever was remaining light was mixed in august new york times and pitchfork while once in a lifetime has become perhaps the band's most popular song it failed to break the billboard top 100 but it did get lots of plays at the famous club paradise
