how shadow puppets work if you hold your hands too close to the light the Shadows are soft and blurry but if you put enough distance between your hands and the light the Shadows are sharp and clear so to recap the difference between a hard and soft light is how it renders shadows and the hardness and softness of a light is controlled by the size and distance relative to the subject so what's the deal with diffusion so diffuse light is often confused for soft light and honestly at this point they're often considered the same thing since usually they're used together but while soft light is primarily defined by the relative size to the subject diffuse light is when light passes through a physical material whether it's gel diffusion particles in air like dust Haze or smoke or pass through silks or Fabrics like muslin all of these diffuse the light meaning they scatter the light spreading it across the entire material and emitting what looks like a more even light an easy way to understand this is to look at a flashlight let's hit the lights so let's shine this flashlight against a flat wall notice there's a very bright spot in the middle that's called a specular highlight and that's caused by the bright bulb in the middle of the flashlight so now if we add some diffusion the specular highlight basically disappears and it spreads out the light evenly over its radius so let's go back to LY and put diffusion on the hard light what the diffusion is doing is evening out the light that's hitting your subject reducing glare and smoothing out the specular highlights the light appears more even across your subject but the edges of your shadows will mostly remain the same But ultimately the onew punch that diffusion can offer is this it can diffuse the light and increase the physical size of your Source at the same time so let's look at this light panel this is a 1ex one Astra it's about square foot in size let's add a soft box to it so now we have a more diffused light and a larger Source this is 24 in across if we want something bigger let's look at this Sola 6 Fel it's about the size of my hand but but if I turn it around and bounce it off of this bounce card now we have a light SZ the entire size of this card and last but not least if you have an 8x8 silk you can turn this light into an 8T wide Source or 64 Square ft so now that we know what hard light and soft light do and how to control them what are we communicating by using them the quality of light alone can tell us an incredible amount of things it can Orient us then there was light give us Clues to where we are what time of day it is a beautiful sunset are we seeing the soft gentle light of dawn or the harsh direct light of midday are we in a hot sweaty City in the summer you're casting dangerous looking Shadows around us in a shady part of town or are we walking along familiar city sidewalks with warm inviting light spilling out from Neighbors Windows is the Smoky hazy going even further it can portray our characters in literally a flattering or unflattering light revealing to the audience who they are are they mysterious and alluring I've been dead once already or are they repulsive and hardened to the world are they deeply in love or are they in deep turmoil do they look youthful and bright or is the wisdom of their years edch deeply into the wrinkles of their face you can say a lot with quality of light and the best part is the same principles in film Lighting on professional sets apply to your lights at home so now that you know how to control the quality of light you can find creative ways to achieve it if you want to create soft light find ways to make your Source bigger use homemade diffusion like a shower curtain or a bed sheet to create a large soft Source in front of your subject and if you want to create hard light use smaller sources or move big powerful sources farther away to get those harsher Shadows so don't be afraid to test things - Ultimate Guide to Cinematic Lighting — Types of Light & Gear Explained [Shot List Ep. 12]: Kind: captions Language: en-US Films are made up of sequences. Sequences are made up of scenes. And scenes are made up of shots. "- That's a kill." - In previous episodes, we covered camera movement, editing techniques, and composition. Today we'll focus on lighting fundamentals from different types and qualities of light to essential lighting terms and gear. "- And then there was the light." - This is Episode 12 of the "Shot List". Cinematic Lighting - Part One. Hit subscribe and enable notifications to get all future episodes of the "Shot List". At the end of the video, we'll explain how you can download your own cheat sheet for everything we're about to cover. "- Let there be light. Ah, my eyes." - Lighting is one of the most important elements of filmmaking. More than just illumination lighting itself plays a massive role in visual storytelling. Lighting can help determine the mood of your scene. Provide insights into a character. A guide to a viewer's eye. On set, these decisions are made primarily by the Director of Photography. and the Gaffer who is the headlighting electrician on the crew. Once a lighting plan has been decided by the DP, the gaffer executes it by obtaining the necessary lights and gear and overseeing the setups. In part one of this video, we'll cover the lighting fundamentals. In part two, we'll dive into how to light subjects and their surroundings. Before you begin setting up lights, it's important to understand the different forms light comes in. "- Explain all to me now." - All light can be split into two categories. Natural and artificial. Natural light is any light that occurs naturally like sunlight, moonlight, and firelight. Artificial light is everything else. Man-made light powered by electricity. From there, we can branch out into additional categories. Ambient lighting refers to any lights that are on location which the crew didn't bring. This includes street lamps, neon signs, or the moon. Practical lighting refers to any light source that can be seen in the frame whether it be ambient or placed there by a gaffer. Motivated lighting means that there is a logical or reasonable justification for the light in a scene. Often practical lights exist simply to justify or motivate the light. To be able to work with light, cinematographers also need to understand its properties. For example, how does one measure light? Lumens is a measurement of how much light a source produces. Lux or Footcandles measures how much of that light hits the subject. Otherwise known as incidental light. Reflective light is the amount of light that reaches the camera. The amount of light is dependent on both the strength of the source and its position in relation to the subject. As soon as light leaves the source it begins to lose intensity. This is called light falloff. And we can predict an account for this loss using an equation called the inverse square law. The intensity of light is equal to one over the distance squared. Basically, this means that every time you double the distance away from the source the light is reduced by 75 percent. Another aspect of light that cinematographers measure is the color temperature which is measured in thermodynamic units called Kelvins. Daylight is bluer than most artificial lights at about 5600 Kelvin. Daylight can also be imitated by artificial light sources with the same temperature. Tungsten lights are a common artificial source with a color temperature of about 3200 Kelvin though this can vary based on the source. Mixing these two light sources therefore can add a variety of color to your shot. With tungsten lights adding orange and daylight adding blue. For a thorough explanation on color temperature, check out our video linked below. Once you understand your light sources, you'll need to know some basic concepts and terminology. To the uninitiated, hearing cinematographers talk about lighting can sound like a foreign language. Let's look at some of the most commonly used terms on set. Exposure is the amount of light that reaches the sensor or film stock. In previous videos, we explained the mechanisms that determine the exposure. The aperture, shutter speed, and ISO. The aperture is an adjustable mechanism that determines how much light enters the lens and the various sizes are measured in f-stops. Each f-stop either doubles the amount of light or reduces it by half. T-stops are another common setting in cinema lenses to measure exactly how much light actually hits the sensor. All cinematography is a balance between light and shadow. And one of the ways this relationship is expressed is called the contrast ratio. This is a shorthand for how we measure the difference between light and shadow in two areas of an image. Usually between the two sides of a subject's face. Or between the subject and the background. High-key lighting is a term that expresses a low contrast ratio where the background and the subject are lit evenly with minimal contrast. Low-key lighting on the other hand uses a high contrast ratio where the difference between light and shadow is much larger with maximum contrast. Both high and low-key lighting are used in a variety of genres to evoke specific moods and atmospheres. Overall high key lighting is used for brighter happier scenes. While low-key lighting is often used for darker dramatic scenes. "- You're sheltering enemies of the state, are you not?" - Chiaroscuro is a type of low-key lighting with a high contrast ratio. This is seen most frequently in film noirs to enhance the sub-genre's mysterious and dangerous qualities. Check out our video on Chiaroscuro to learn more. A cinematographer must also choose between hard light and soft light. Hard light is more directional light that creates stark shadows which works best in more dramatic or intense scenes. Soft light is more diffused and generates less defined shadows. This type of lighting is often used for happier or romantic scenes. Some of the best naturally occurring soft light comes during magic hour or golden hour. This occurs twice a day just before sunset. And just after sunrise when the sunlight is less harsh and more colorful. Lighting placement in relation to the camera should also be considered. The dumb side also called the broadside is lighting the side of a subject which is closer to the camera often giving the subject a more approachable feeling. The smart side also called the short side refers to lighting the side of a subject that is not facing the camera giving the subject a more dramatic look. Lens flares are created when light hits the lens. The shapes and colors of which differ depending on the lens. "- For me, it literally is just this aesthetic thing that I like which I absolutely am guilty of overusing, and realize that it can be insanely distracting but I do love the look of it. And it's just this incredible look that for some moments feel appropriate." - Bokeh are out-of-focus elements in a shot. Typically points of light that often add a romantic or painterly texture to the image. Now that we've got these key terms and concepts under our belts let's look at the gear necessary to achieve them. "- Yes, we'll find expensive gear you brought out here, Mr. Hooper." - A professional film set often has an expansive variety of lighting equipment but there's some gear which are almost certain to see on any set. Lights themselves come in a variety of forms. There are tungsten lights which are powerful and have a warm color temperature. Daylight is usually achieved with HMI lights which are even stronger but have a cooler temperature. Fluorescent lights which offer soft cool light. Kino Flos which are banks of tube lights adjustable to various temperatures. And LED lights which provide a high amount of control over color and strength. For more control of a light's color, gaffers will often use gels. Thin plastic sheets which come in an assortment of colors. Used in concert with a light's color temperature gels help cinematographers achieve their desired hues. A light meter is used to measure the amount of incidental lighting hitting a subject which then informs the exposure settings on the camera. Some models also include a spot meter which allows you to pinpoint an exact part of the image for exposure. Many cameras themselves also have different modes to measure the reflective light actually reaching the lens. Other ways to measure lighting ratios include waveforms, histograms, and false color which we'll cover in a future video. Once you've made the choice to use soft light in the scene, there are many ways to create the necessary diffusion. Diffusion is when a semi-translucent material is placed between the light source and the subject. This scatters the light making it softer and there are a few methods used to do this. Softboxes use translucent cloths placed in front of a light. Diffusion paper can achieve a similar effect. Silks are often used for larger sources. Of course, there are also natural diffusers like clouds, fog, dust, or smoke. Bouncing the light with reflectors creates diffusion while also redirecting light. Reflectors come in a variety of colors and textures each bringing a unique type of diffusion. Bead boards are styrofoam boards with imperfect surfaces that scatter light differently than a piece of foam core which bounces light more evenly. Another way to mitigate the intensity of the light is with neutral-density gels. These can be attached to the light or applied to windows to reduce the sunlight. Flags are opaque boards that shape and block unwanted light. Barn doors are adjustable flaps on the light itself which have a similar function. Egg crates direct light and help prevent it from spilling into unwanted areas. Cookies add texture to a light. Short for the term 'Cucoloris' cookies are boards with patterns cut into them that create a dappled effect when placed in front of a light. Mounting and positioning lights on set can be done with light stands or with c-stands which are also useful for setting up diffusion, flags, and reflectors. Just about anything needed to complete the desired setup. Believe it or not, we've just scratched the surface on cinematic lighting. "- This is only the beginning." - There are more terms, gear, and concepts out there with more arriving every day. In the next episode, we'll take everything we learned here and apply it to real-life lighting scenarios including how to light a subject versus how to light the background. With actual lighting setups from the best cinematographers working today. In the description, you'll find a link to the StudioBinder blog where you can download a PDF Cheatsheet with everything we've covered in this video and more. That's all for now. We hope we've illuminated these lighting fundamentals properly. Stay tuned for part two. We'll see you there.

- Camera Lenses Explained: What Every Beginner NEEDS to Know: Kind: captions Language: en so camera lenses can be super confusing at first like what do all of these numbers mean on the lens what's the are designed to bend light to focus the light on hitting your camera sensor at a particular spot thus creating your image so without a lens your DSLR or mirorless camera is practically useless now all cameras leverage a specific mounting system to attach your lens to your camera body and each brand is going to have its own lens mounting system so Sony right here uses the e-mount system so these cameras can only take lenses that are made for the e-mount and this is whether or not you're buying a Sony branded lens or thirdparty lenses from companies like Tamron Sigma or here's a VT lens right here and it's an e-mount lens so I can screw this directly on to a Sony e-mount camera and it works just fine now what's weird is a company like Canon for their older dslrs use the EF Mount while their newer line of mirrorless cameras use the RF Mount so even within the same camera company there's sometimes different mounts that these cameras use so you have to make sure that you purchase the right lens for the right camera there are some lens mount converters out there but there are quite a few trade-offs when you start trying to like put a Canon lens on a Sony body and stuff so I would typically steer clear of that and just purchase the appropriate lens for your camera type now one of the most important factors for any lens is its focal length and every lens has a focal length that's expressed in millimeters and you'll see this on the lens as mm you might see 20 millim or 50 mm or even a range like 70 to 200 millim these are all different focal lengths now focal lengths are important for a few different reasons and the first is that it determines your field of view otherwise known as f V so this is how much of your scene is going to be captured how wide is your shot going to be or how tight will your shot be focal lengths with a wider fov are things like 11 mm 16 mm and 20 mm and these are great for showcasing wide scenes like Landscapes or buildings or even recording in smaller Studio spaces like I'm in right now I'm actually using a 20 mm lens in my loft Studio space now focal lengths with a medium field of view are things like 35 mm or 5050 or 70 mm and these are great for everyday shooting and just filling a variety of needs and then focal lengths with a tighter field of view are things like 85 mm or 105 or 200 mm and Beyond these are known as telephoto lenses and these are great for capturing subjects that are further away from your camera like Wildlife photography or Sports content or concerts and more so that's how your focal length affects your different fields of view now focal link will also determine your lens compression and compression is the effect where objects in the background appear to almost be closer to your subject when you're using longer focal lengths like telephoto lenses and this happens because Tel photo lenses narrow your field of view like we talked about but they also magnify distant objects and they make the background seem larger and closer to your subject than a wide angle lens would so just know that when you use a longer focal length your image is going to look more compressed now the third main factor that focal length affects is lens Distortion and wider focal lengths will tend to be a little bit more distorted and they're going to make the world seem almost like stretched out or expanded sometimes even straight lines like buildings are going to start looking curved with these wider focal lengths and then tighter focal lengths on the other hand tend to minimize Distortion keeping lines more straight and overall your image will just look more natural when you use these tighter lenses now all lenses fall under two different categories you have prime lenses and you have zoom lenses and prime lenses have a fixed focal length so their focal lengths cannot be changed so a 50 mm lens like this is only going to give you that 50 m field of view and if you want to get closer or even wider from your subject you have to physically move your camera to recompose your image they also usually have fewer elements inside of them and usually can produce sharper images than zoom lenses and finally they also usually have the ability to have a wider open aperture value for shooting in low light situations and just getting a shallower depth of field now zoom lenses on the other hand can vary their focal length by adjusting different lens elements with zoom so a common Zoom range with a zoom lens would be something like this this is a 24 to 70 Zoom or you might see a 70 to 200 telephoto lens but there's a wide range of zoom lenses out there zoom lenses are certainly more versatile than prime lenses for quickly getting different types of shots like wides mids and tights but these lenses are also often less sharp than prime lenses because they have more elements and more complexity to them they also often have an aperture that doesn't open as wide as a prime lens does and it's common to see a maximum aperture on a zoom lens be f2.8 or 3.5 or F4 which will just let in a little bit less light is one of these style of lenses better than the other well not really it kind of just depends on what you're going for if you want the sharpest possible results then you probably would need to use a prime lens if you want more versatility and you're more on the go and you need to recompose different kinds of shots quickly then You' go for a zoom lens it all just kind of depends on your specific needs and your needs might change depending on what you're shooting now let's talk about aperture and aperture controls how much light enters your camera through the lens and also affects your depth of field and measured in what's called f stops like f1.8 or F16 and you'll typically see these f- stop values actually on your lens most of the time a lower f-stop number like f1.8 creates a really wide aperture opening which lets in a ton of light for your camera sensor which particularly can help in lower light situations and an aperture of 1.8 is also going to produce a really shallow depth of field so this is going to give you that really nice blurry background for beautiful looking shots it's great for isolating a subject from the background or for YouTube videos like this one right here or even doing product b-roll in photography getting that shallow depth of field is a really sought after look now a higher f-stop number like F16 actually creates a really small aperture opening on your lens and that's going to let in less light so this can actually be helpful on bright sunny days to cut out light from hitting your sensor so you can actually get correct exposure if it's too bright outside but this is also going to produce a wider depth of field so it's going to keep keep more of your scene in Focus this can be really helpful for shooting landscape photography or video or really just any situation in which you want more of your frame in Focus now lenses can either have a constant aperture or a variable aperture now constant aperture lenses will apply to all prime lenses as well as more expensive lenses that are designed so that when you zoom in your aperture isn't going to change values at all but variable aperture lenses on the other hand change their aperture as you zoom into tighter focal lengths and this is especially common on the standard kit lenses that are shipped with most of these cameras because it's just more affordable to make lenses in this fashion so as you check out this kit lens that I put on my Sony camera as you zoom in from 18 millim to 55 millim my aperture is actually going to automatically stop down from 3.5 to 5.6 which reduces the amount of light hitting my sensor so it's really important to know whether your lens has a constant aperture or not because it actually is going to affect the rest of your camera settings now let's talk about the difference between a fullframe lens and an apsc or crop sensor lens so some lenses are designed to work with a larger sensor like a fullframe sensor and then others are designed to work with smaller apsc crop sensor format cameras and what's really key about this distinction is that when you're using an apsc camera regardless of the lens you put on it whether it's a fullframe design lens or an apsc your effective field of view is actually going to change because of the size of the sensor so this is known as crop factor maybe you've heard this phrase before which refers to the multiplier that you'll add to whatever lens you're shooting on when you're using an apsc camera now if that confuses you you're not alone let's break it down really simply so take this 20 mm lens for example on a full-frame camera this 20 mm lens would give you a true 20 mm field of view makes sense right it's 20 millim it should be 20 but when you put the same lens on a crop sensor camera you're going to multiply the focal length by 1.5x and that will be the case on Sony and Nikon and Fuji and most of the Brands except for Canon which Canon is weird and you would multiply it by 1.6 times on all of Canon's cameras so that means by putting a 20 mm lens on a apsc camera you're actually getting a 30 mm field of view because of the difference in sensor size so the main takeaway here is if you like the wide field of view you get when you're shooting on a fullframe camera if you want that same field of view you're going to have to shoot on even wider lenses on apsc so something like 10 mm or 16 mm is going to get you that wider look when you're shooting on crop sensor cameras what's also interesting is I shoot on Sony cameras and you can actually place any fullframe lens on a crop sensor camera and it's going to work just fine but when I put an apsc lens on my fullframe camera you can see quite a bit of vetting around the edges because that lens isn't designed to shoot with such a large sensor so you're going to get some vignetting when you do that most full-frame cameras do actually have an apsc crop mode that you can turn the camera into and now you're able to use apsc lenses more effectively and if any of that just confused you please drop a comment down below and we be sure to help you out all righty so now we're going to talk about all things focusing starting with autofocus versus manual focus so not every lens is going to have autofocus especially really large Cinema lenses or even older vintage lenses maybe you have like a film camera that you're adapting the lens from that lens might not have autofocus nowadays though most lenses actually do have autofocus capabilities so you don't have to manual focus your lenses most of the time you're going to use autofocus for recording video or taking photos but sometimes manually focusing your lens is nice because it can ensure that your focus position is not going to change no matter what is happening in your camera frame I actually use manual focus probably on like a weekly basis when I'm recording b-roll because there are times where I feel like my camera's autofocus is almost too fast or too Snappy and sometimes swapping into manual focus can just give me more control over what my focus is doing there's also something called minimum focusing distance and every lens has a closest distance that it's able to focus on and if your subject is closer than this minimum focusing distance your camera won't be able to focus on that subject anymore so for example this 24 to70 soning lens has a minimum Focus distance of 0.38 M or 1.25 ft so you'll see this number on your lens most of the time so in order for this lens to be in Focus I have to be a minimum of 1.25 ft away in order for it to actually hit Focus so just take a look at whatever lens you have and check out its minimum focusing distance and if you need a lens that is able to focus really close on a subject then you're going to want to look at what is called a macro lens to get those really close detail shots and the last Focus thing I'll talk about is focus breathing so some lenses especially ones that are a little bit more cheaply made experience what's called Focus breathing and this is kind of a slight change in a lens's field of view when you change your focus position so as you shift between really close and far subjects your frame almost might look like it's subtly zooming in or out which can be kind of confusing if you don't know what's going on while it's probably the focus breathing happening with your lens it's especially noticeable when you're recording video and it can actually be pretty distracting to the viewer so ideally you want to minimize this if you can some cameras like my Sony a74 for example have a setting called Focus breathing compensation where it will use software to correct for some of that Focus breathing which is actually a pretty cool setting and you can turn that on if your camera has it otherwise if you're using a lens that has really bad Focus breathing you can swap it into manual focus and that will eliminate Focus breathing entirely so those are a few really important focusing features you need to know about your camera lens now in terms of getting stable footage or photos some lenses actually feature lens stabilization which can help reduce vibrations and micro Jitters that you get when operating a camera so this can be especially helpful when you're shooting handheld and you just want to still have decently smooth footage some lenses actually even have a physical switch where you can turn lens stabilization on and off really quickly if you don't want to use it which is pretty neat and an important note here is that not every lens features lens stabilization and you will usually have to pay a little bit more money when buying a lens that has this feature and just so you know lens stabilization is different from Ibis or inbody image stabilization on your camera so you actually can have one without the other your lens might have stabilization but your camera doesn't have inbody stabilization and vice versa or you can sometimes have both and that's going to give you super stable footage now most lenses will also come with a lens Hood which will screw on to the front of your lens to block out unwanted light from hitting your camera sensor so this actually can be really helpful if you are recording outdoors and the sun is shining really brightly and maybe coming towards your lens sometimes that can produce an unwanted camera flare so screwing on your lens Hood can really help with that also on the front of your camera lens are some threads so you can screw on filters to your lens so on your lens usually you will see a logo indicating the thread size of the lens that you're using so this number right here say 82 means that you'll need to purchase an 82 mm filter to attach to this lens there are also things called Step Up or step down rings that allow you to convert the thread size of your lens to a higher or lower number so that you can actually use different size filters with your lens this is actually a little hack that I use sometimes if I only want to buy one specific filter and I don't want to buy that one filter for every size lens that I have then I'll just buy some step down rings and adapt the filter to my smaller lenses and this saves me money which I love saving money so that's how thread sizes work we'll link some filters and step up rings down in the description below so with all of this information what is a lens that you might want to pick up first well typically the easiest lens to get as your first lens would be a kit lens these are typically the lenses that are shipped with these different camera bodies and they're usually pretty cheap you can get them for about $100 maybe $150 add-on to your camera when you purchase it and that's a pretty good starting point if you're ready to upgrade your lenses a little bit and you're on the apsc format the two lenses that I'd recommend you look at first are the sigma 16 mm f1.4 lens which is a beautiful lens that'll let in a bunch of light for shooting in lower light situations and also with that lower aperture value is going to give you a really shallow depth of field for beautiful looking shots there's also the Sigma 18 to 50 mm f2.8 lens and that Conant 2.8 aperture is going to be really helpful for not having to change your exposure so much when you're zooming in and out and that 18 to 50 mm range will be a really versatile option for the apsc format if you're on fullframe camera systems one of my favorite lenses is this vro 20 mm f2.8 fullframe lens it's super budget as well it's like $158 or $170 depending on when you buy it it's really budget
